Chapter C
Supplement to Steering Exposure

1
No-go theorem for multi-black-box universal steering bits

In contrast to the protocols exploring the
capabilities of wirings within the
AB
partition, in this section
we present a no-go theorem limiting their transformation power. Since
it is known
[
122
]
that in minimal dimension there is
no steering bit — i.e. no “universal” minimal-dimension assemblage
that can be transformed into any other under 1W-LOCCs — one can
ask whether reduction from a higher number of inputs, outputs or parties
allows such a steering bit to be established. We answer in the negative
even in minimal dimension.

Theorem 2
.

[No pure steering bit with higher number of parties]
There does not exist any pure
(N-1)
-DI qubit
assemblage
\sigma_{\bm{a}|\bm{x}}^{\text{bit}}
,
where
\bm{a}=\{a_{1},...,a_{N-1}\}
,
\bm{x}=\{x_{1},...,x_{N-1}\}

(with finite sets of input and output values), that can be transformed
via 1W-LOCCs into all qubit assemblages of minimal dimension
\sigma_{a|x}^{(\text{target})}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar in spirit to that of Theorem 5
of
[
122
]
. We consider a pure
(N-1)
-DI qubit assemblage
as a candidate for higher-dimensional “bit” assemblage. With the
more detailed notation of
[
122
]
, it reads

\displaystyle\sigma_{\bm{a}|\bm{x}}^{\text{bit}}=P_{\bm{A}|\bm{X}}(\bm{a}|\bm{x})\ |\psi({\bm{a},\bm{x}})\rangle\langle\psi({\bm{a},\bm{x}})|\ .

(253)

We assume the NS principle only between the DD party and all others,
the
N-1
DI parties may signal to each other at will. We will show
that no single choice of
\sigma_{\bm{a}|\bm{x}}^{\text{bit}}

can be freely transformed into members of a family of minimal-dimension
assemblages
\sigma_{a_{f}|x_{f}}^{\theta}=\frac{1}{2}|\psi^{\theta}(a_{f},x_{f})\rangle\langle\psi^{\theta}(a_{f},x_{f})|

for all
\theta\in{}]0,\pi/2[{}
, where

\displaystyle|\psi^{\theta}(0,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=|0\rangle

(254a)

\displaystyle|\psi^{\theta}(1,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=|1\rangle

(254b)

\displaystyle|\psi^{\theta}(0,1)\rangle

\displaystyle=\ \ \cos\theta|0\rangle+\sin\theta|1\rangle

(254c)

\displaystyle|\psi^{\theta}(1,1)\rangle

\displaystyle=-\sin\theta|0\rangle+\cos\theta|1\rangle\ .

(254d)

The most general form
of a 1W-LOCC applied to
\sigma_{\bm{a}|\bm{x}}^{\text{bit}}

is

\sum_{\bm{a},\bm{x},\omega}P_{\bm{X}|X_{f},\Omega}^{\theta}(\bm{x}|x_{f},\omega)\ P_{A_{f}|\bm{A},\bm{X},\Omega,X_{f}}^{\theta}(a_{f}|\bm{a},\bm{x},\omega,x_{f})\ P_{\bm{A}|\bm{X}}(\bm{a}|\bm{x})\ K_{\omega}^{\theta}|\psi({\bm{a},\bm{x}})\rangle\langle\psi({\bm{a},\bm{x}})|K_{\omega}^{\theta\dagger}\ ,

(255)

where
\Omega
is a variable (with values
\omega
) representing
information sent by the quantum party to the classical ones,
P_{\bm{X}|X_{f},\Omega}^{\theta}

and
P_{A_{f}|\bm{A},\bm{X},\Omega,X_{f}}^{\theta}

are conditional probability distributions, and
K_{\omega}^{\theta}

is a Kraus operator
[
122
]
; the three may depend on
\theta
.
Since this transformed assemblage is intended to equal the rank-1
assemblage
\sigma_{a_{f}|x_{f}}^{\theta}
, we can conclude that

\forall\ a_{f},x_{f}

\sum_{\bm{a},\bm{x}}P_{\bm{X}|X_{f},\Omega}^{\theta}(\bm{x}|x_{f},\omega)P_{A_{f}|\bm{A},\bm{X},\Omega,X_{f}}^{\theta}(a_{f}|\bm{a},\bm{x},\omega,x_{f})P_{\bm{A}|\bm{X}}(\bm{a}|\bm{x})\ K_{\omega}^{\theta}|\psi({\bm{a},\bm{x}})\rangle\langle\psi({\bm{a},\bm{x}})|K_{\omega}^{\theta\dagger}\\
\sim|\psi^{\theta}(a_{f},x_{f})\rangle\langle\psi^{\theta}(a_{f},x_{f})|\ ,

(256)

where
\sim
signifies “is either null or proportional to” and
we have used the fact that the relation, valid for the sum in
\omega
,
is also valid for each
\omega
term.

We will assume for now
that
\sigma_{\bm{a}|\bm{x}}^{\text{bit}}
is not
a single-state assemblage, i.e., there is no state
|\psi_{\text{single}}\rangle

such that
|\psi(\bm{a},\bm{x})\rangle=|\psi_{\text{single}}\rangle

for all
\bm{a},\bm{x}
(for our purposes throughout
this proof, states are equal if they differ only by an global phase).

We
now notice that, due to normalization,
\forall\ x_{f},\omega
,
\exists\ \tilde{\bm{x}},\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{a}_{f}

such that
P_{\bm{X}|X_{f},\Omega}^{\theta}(\tilde{\bm{x}}|x_{f},\omega)\times P_{A_{f}|\bm{A},\bm{X},\Omega,X_{f}}^{\theta}(\tilde{a}_{f}|\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}},\omega,x_{f})\times P_{\bm{A}|\bm{X}}(\tilde{\bm{a}}|\tilde{\bm{x}})\neq 0
.
For these values, then,

K_{\omega}^{\theta}|\psi(\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}})\rangle\sim|\psi^{\theta}(\tilde{a}_{f},x_{f})\rangle\ .

(257)

In fact, there must be at least two different values
\tilde{\bm{a}}

for each
\tilde{\bm{x}}
for which Eq. (
257
)
is true, with the corresponding pure states
|\psi(\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}})\rangle

being not all equal: if, for some
\tilde{\bm{x}}
there is a single
\tilde{\bm{a}}

with
P_{\bm{A}|\bm{X}}(\tilde{\bm{a}}|\tilde{\bm{x}})\neq 0
,
then by purity and the NS property between the DD and DI partitions,

\sigma_{\bm{a}|\bm{x}}^{\text{bit}}
would be a
single-state assemblage; if for all values
\tilde{\bm{a}}
,

|\psi(\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}})\rangle
is the
same, it would also be a single-state assemblage due to NS and purity.

Let
us now exclude the possibility of
K_{\omega}^{\theta}|\psi(\bm{a},\bm{x})\rangle=0

with
K_{\omega}^{\theta}\neq 0
. If that were the case,
K_{\omega}^{\theta}

would have a rank-1 support, hence a rank-1 span:
K_{\omega}^{\theta}|\psi(\bm{a},\bm{x})\rangle\sim|k_{\omega}^{\theta}\rangle\ \forall\ \bm{a},\bm{x}
.
From (
256
) and the independence of
x_{f}
from

\omega
, this would require either
|\psi^{\theta}(a_{f},0)\rangle\propto|k_{\omega}^{\theta}\rangle\propto|\psi^{\theta}(\tilde{a}_{f},1)\rangle

[contradiction with Eq. (
254
)] or that, for some
value of
x_{f}
, for the corresponding
\tilde{\bm{x}}
,

K_{\omega}^{\theta}|\psi(\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}})\rangle=0

for all
\tilde{\bm{a}}
with
P_{\bm{A}|\bm{X}}(\tilde{\bm{a}}|\tilde{\bm{x}})\neq 0

[contradiction with there existing two different states
|\psi(\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}})\rangle
].

Finally,
we can conclude from the dependencies of the three probabilities
P_{\bm{X}|X_{f},\Omega}^{\theta},

P_{A_{f}|\bm{A},\bm{X},\Omega,X_{f}}^{\theta},

P_{\bm{A}|\bm{X}}
on
x_{f},\omega,\bm{x},\bm{a},\tilde{a}_{f}
,
that

K_{\omega}^{\theta}|\psi(\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}})\rangle\propto|\psi^{\theta}(\tilde{a}_{f},x_{f})\rangle\ .

(258)

The validity conditions of this equation are as follows: for all
(x_{f},\omega)
,
there exists some value
\tilde{\bm{x}}
for which (
258
)
holds; for each
\tilde{\bm{x}}
, there are at least two
values
\tilde{\bm{a}}
for which (
258
) holds;
and for each choice of
(x_{f},\omega,\tilde{\bm{x}},\tilde{\bm{a}})

there is some value
\tilde{a}_{f}
for which (
258
)
holds. Moreover, for given
\tilde{\bm{x}}
, the corresponding

|\psi(\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}})\rangle
(for
varying
\tilde{\bm{a}}
) are not all equal.

Let us
explore the possible ways of satisfying Eq. (
258
)
by case analysis. A first possibility is that, for the two different
values
x_{f}=0,1
, the values of
\tilde{\bm{x}}
for which
(
258
) holds intersect at some value
\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\text{int}}
.
Then
\exists\ \tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{a}_{f0},\tilde{a}_{f1}

such that

\begin{split}K_{\omega}^{\theta}|\psi(\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\text{int}})\rangle&\propto|\psi^{\theta}(\tilde{a}_{f0},x_{f}=0)\rangle\ ,\\
K_{\omega}^{\theta}|\psi(\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\text{int}})\rangle&\propto|\psi^{\theta}(\tilde{a}_{f1},x_{f}=1)\rangle\ ,\end{split}

(259)

which is incompatible with Eq. (
254
). We are then
left with the values
\tilde{\bm{x}}
for
x_{f}=0
and

x_{f}=1
being all different. Taking the liberty to relabel our
variables, let us consider a value
\tilde{\bm{x}}=\bm{0}

for
x_{f}=0
and a value
\tilde{\bm{x}}=\bm{1}

for
x_{f}=1
, ignoring the other possible values of
\tilde{\bm{x}}

for which Eq. (
258
) holds. Let us call
\tilde{\bm{a}}=\bm{0}

and
\tilde{\bm{a}}=\bm{1}
the two values of
\tilde{\bm{a}}

for which, given
\tilde{\bm{x}}
, Eq. (
258
)
holds. We see that
\tilde{a}_{f}
could take any value for each

\tilde{\bm{a}}
. However, if
\tilde{a}_{f}
is the same
for the same
(x_{f},\tilde{\bm{x}})
and two different
\tilde{\bm{a}}
,
e.g.,

\begin{split}&K_{\omega}|\psi(\bm{0},\bm{1})\rangle\propto|\psi^{\theta}(0,1)\rangle\\
&K_{\omega}|\psi(\bm{1},\bm{1})\rangle\propto|\psi^{\theta}(0,1)\rangle\ ,\end{split}

(260)

then Eq. (
258
) cannot be satisfied for all
x_{f}
.
This is because
\{|\psi(\bm{0},\bm{1})\rangle,|\psi(\bm{1},\bm{1})\rangle\}

form a basis of the qubit Hilbert space, hence
K_{\omega}
has a
1-rank span given by
|\psi^{\theta}(0,1)\rangle
, which does not span

|\psi^{\theta}(\tilde{a}_{f},0)\rangle
as needed. Hence
\tilde{a}_{f}

is different for each
\tilde{\bm{a}}
value.

We can
then conclude that, up to relabeling, there must be states
|\psi(\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}})\rangle

belonging to
\bm{\sigma}^{\text{bit}}
which obey

\displaystyle K_{\omega}|\psi(\bm{0},\bm{0})\rangle\propto|\psi^{\theta}(0,0)\rangle

(261a)

\displaystyle K_{\omega}|\psi(\bm{1},\bm{0})\rangle\propto|\psi^{\theta}(1,0)\rangle

(261b)

\displaystyle K_{\omega}|\psi(\bm{0},\bm{1})\rangle\propto|\psi^{\theta}(0,1)\rangle

(261c)

\displaystyle K_{\omega}|\psi(\bm{1},\bm{1})\rangle\propto|\psi^{\theta}(1,1)\rangle

(261d)

to obtain the family of assemblages

\{\bm{\sigma}^{\theta}\}_{\theta\in{}]0,\pi/2[{}}
. We will
choose the following parametrization:

|\psi(\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}})\rangle=\cos(\varphi_{\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}}})|0\rangle+e^{i\alpha_{\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}}}}\sin(\varphi_{\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}}})|1\rangle\ ,

(262)

where
\varphi_{\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}}}\in[0,\pi/2]
.
It should be noted that
(\varphi_{\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}}},\alpha_{\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}}})

may depend on
\theta
through
\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}}
:
because
P_{\bm{X}|X_{f},\Omega}^{\theta}
may depend on

\theta
, the values
\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}}

for which Eq. (
258
) holds may vary for different
values of
\theta
. However, for finitely many values of
\bm{a}
,

\bm{x}
, there are only finitely many states and finitely
many
(\varphi_{{\bm{a}},{\bm{x}}},\alpha_{{\bm{a}},{\bm{x}}})

to pick from, so some choice of states as in Eq. (
262
)
must still be able to satisfy Eq. (
261
) for a continuous
set of values
\theta
.

Substituting Eqs. (
254
)
and (
262
) in (
261a
,
261b
),
respectively, we see that

\frac{K_{\omega 00}^{\theta}}{K_{\omega 01}^{\theta}}=-e^{i\alpha_{\bm{1}\bm{0}}}\tan\varphi_{\bm{1}\bm{0}};\ \frac{K_{\omega 10}^{\theta}}{K_{\omega 11}^{\theta}}=-e^{i\alpha_{\bm{0}\bm{0}}}\tan\varphi_{\bm{0}\bm{0}};

(263)

where
K_{\omega ij}^{\theta}:=\langle i|K_{\omega}^{\theta}|j\rangle
.
Doing the same in (
261c
,
261d
) and substituting
(
263
), we find, respectively,

\displaystyle\frac{K_{\omega 11}^{\theta}}{K_{\omega 01}^{\theta}}

\displaystyle=\tan\theta\frac{\tan\varphi_{\bm{0}\bm{1}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{0}\bm{1}}}-\tan\varphi_{\bm{1}\bm{0}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{1}\bm{0}}}}{\tan\varphi_{\bm{0}\bm{1}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{0}\bm{1}}}+\tan\varphi_{\bm{0}\bm{0}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{0}\bm{0}}}}

(264)

\displaystyle\frac{K_{\omega 11}^{\theta}}{K_{\omega 01}^{\theta}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\tan\theta}\frac{\tan\varphi_{\bm{1}\bm{1}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{1}\bm{1}}}-\tan\varphi_{\bm{1}\bm{0}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{1}\bm{0}}}}{\tan\varphi_{\bm{1}\bm{1}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{1}\bm{1}}}-\tan\varphi_{\bm{0}\bm{0}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{0}\bm{0}}}}.

(265)

Equating the two, we have

\begin{split}\tan^{2}\theta\left(\frac{\tan\varphi_{\bm{0}\bm{1}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{0}\bm{1}}}-\tan\varphi_{\bm{1}\bm{0}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{1}\bm{0}}}}{\tan\varphi_{\bm{0}\bm{1}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{0}\bm{1}}}+\tan\varphi_{\bm{0}\bm{0}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{0}\bm{0}}}}\right)++\left(\frac{\tan\varphi_{\bm{1}\bm{1}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{1}\bm{1}}}-\tan\varphi_{\bm{1}\bm{0}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{1}\bm{0}}}}{\tan\varphi_{\bm{1}\bm{1}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{1}\bm{1}}}-\tan\varphi_{\bm{0}\bm{0}}e^{i\alpha_{\bm{0}\bm{0}}}}\right)=0\ ,\end{split}

(266)

which, for fixed
\varphi_{\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}}},\alpha_{\tilde{\bm{a}},\tilde{\bm{x}}}
,
must hold for a continuous set of values
\theta
. This is only possible
if both parentheses are zero, which in turn implies
(\varphi_{\bm{0},\bm{1}},\alpha_{\bm{0},\bm{1}})=(\varphi_{\bm{1},\bm{0}},\alpha_{\bm{1},\bm{0}})=(\varphi_{\bm{1},\bm{1}},\alpha_{\bm{1},\bm{1}})
,
or
|\psi(\bm{0},\bm{1})\rangle=|\psi(\bm{1},\bm{0})\rangle=|\psi(\bm{1},\bm{1})\rangle
,
contradicting the established relation
|\psi(\bm{0},\bm{1})\rangle\neq|\psi(\bm{1},\bm{1})\rangle
.
This concludes the demonstration for non-single-state assemblages.

Finally,
let us show that a single-state assemblage is unable to do the task.
From (
255
),

\begin{split}\sum_{\bm{a},\bm{x}}P_{\bm{X}|X_{f},\Omega}^{\theta}(\bm{x}|x_{f},\omega)&P_{A_{f}|\bm{A},\bm{X},\Omega,X_{f}}^{\theta}(a_{f}|\bm{a},\bm{x},\omega,x_{f})P_{\bm{A}|\bm{X}}(\bm{a}|\bm{x})\times\\
&\times K_{\omega}^{\theta}|\psi_{\text{single}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\text{single}}|K_{\omega}^{\theta\dagger}\sim|\psi^{\theta}(a_{f},x_{f})\rangle\langle\psi^{\theta}(a_{f},x_{f})|\ .\end{split}

(267)

The sum on the left-hand side is not zero for at least two pairs

(a_{f},x_{f})
, hence
K_{\omega}^{\theta}|\psi_{\text{single}}\rangle

must be proportional to
|\psi^{\theta}(a_{f},x_{f})\rangle
for both
these pairs. This is incompatible with Eq. (
254
),
since none of the
|\psi^{\theta}(a_{f},x_{f})\rangle
are proportional
to one another. ∎

2
Redefinition of genuinely multipartite steering

Although our discussion has focused
on steering along a fixed bipartition, it has a bearing on genuine
multipartite steering as well. This concept hinges on bi-separability
over all possible bipartitions, as used by D. Cavalcanti
et
al
to define genuine multipartite steering in
[
143
]
.
Interestingly, however, our results can be used to generalize that
definition.

Redefinition of genuinely multipartite steering:
An
assemblage
\bm{\sigma}
is genuinely multipartite steerable
if it does not
admit a decomposition of the form

\displaystyle\sigma_{a,b|x,y}=\sum_{\mu}p_{\mu}^{A|BC}

\displaystyle P_{a|x;\mu}

\displaystyle\sigma_{b|y}^{C}(\mu)

(268a)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\nu}p_{\nu}^{B|AC}

\displaystyle P_{b|y;\nu}

\displaystyle\sigma_{a|x}^{C}(\nu)

(268b)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\lambda}p_{\lambda}^{AB|C}

\displaystyle P_{a,b|x,y,\lambda}\

\displaystyle\varrho^{C}(\lambda)

(268c)

where the
last sum can be any TO-LHS assemblage.

The difference from D. Cavalcanti
et al
’s definition is that
they consider assemblages obtained from a quantum realization with
bi-separable states. Reproducing Eqs. (4,5,6) of
[
143
]
,
a tripartite state
\varrho^{ABC}
is bi-separable when decomposable
as

\displaystyle\varrho^{ABC}=\sum_{\mu}p_{\mu}^{A|BC}

\displaystyle\ \varrho_{\mu}^{A}

\displaystyle\otimes\varrho_{\mu}^{BC}

(269a)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\nu}p_{\nu}^{B|AC}

\displaystyle\ \varrho_{\nu}^{B}

\displaystyle\otimes\varrho_{\nu}^{AC}

(269b)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\lambda}p_{\lambda}^{AB|C}

\displaystyle\ \varrho_{\lambda}^{AB}

\displaystyle\otimes\varrho_{\lambda}^{C}\ .

(269c)

Under local measurements
on the
A
and
B
partitions, this yields a 2DI+1DD assemblage
of the form (
268
) (akin to Eqs. (7,8,9)
of
[
143
]
), but with a distribution
P_{a,b|x,y,\lambda}

in Eq. (
268c
) necessarily quantum-realizable
(a subset of NS distributions). In other words, they only allow the
sum in Eq. (
268c
) to be quantum-realizable
NS-LHS assemblages. Our redefinition, then, reduces the set of genuinely
multipartite steerable assemblages.

Morover, we show in Section
3
that there are,
in fact, quantum-realizable assemblages affected by this change. These
assemblages are decomposable as in Eq. (
268
)
only with a TO-LHS (not NS-LHS) term in Eq. (
268c
),
and hence their quantum realization requires genuinely multipartite
entangled states [i.e. not decomposable as Eq. (
269
)].
Interestingly, in this case genuine multipartite entanglement is certified
in the semi-DI scenario without steering: the need for a TO-LHS term
in Eq. (
268c
) implies the inexistence
of a bi-separable decomposition (
269
) for the
underlying quantum state, and also implies unsteerability.

3
On the sets of LHS assemblages, TO-LHS assemblages, and NS-LHS assemblages

a

b

x

y

\sigma_{a,b|x,y}^{W}

0

0

0

0

\frac{1}{6}\left[2\eta^{2}|0\rangle\langle 0|+(1+\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}-\eta^{2}/2)|1\rangle\langle 1|+\eta(1+\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}})X\right]

0

1

0

0

\frac{1}{6}\left[2(1-\eta^{2})|0\rangle\langle 0|+\eta^{2}/2|1\rangle\langle 1|-\eta\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}X\right]

1

Example quantum assemblage to demonstrate strict inclusion of

in

.

We now state a theorem that sustains Fig.
39
b), concerning
the inclusion relations between the sets NS-LHS, TO-LHS, and LHS.

Theorem 3
.

NS-LHS
\subset
TO-LHS
\subset
LHS, and these relations
also hold strictly if we restrict to quantum-realizable assemblages.

Proof.

From the definitions in Eqs. (
122
,
25
),
it is clear that NS-LHS
\subseteq
TO-LHS
\subseteq
LHS. The
phenomenon of exposure implies that the assemblages in Eqs. (
137a
,
141
)
belong to LHS, but not to TO-LHS, so the inclusion of one in the other
is strict (notice that assemblage (
) is
quantum realizable). To prove that NS-LHS is a strict subset of TO-LHS,
we need an example of a TO-LHS assemblage that does not belong to
NS-LHS. One way to do so is to follow the reasoning of
[
127
]
:
take the time-ordered decomposition of the distribution
\bm{P}

from
[
192
]
that violates the guess-your-neighbor’s-input
(GYNI) inequality and find the
\varrho_{\lambda}
that best mimic
the marginal
P_{a|x,\lambda}
— this effectively amounts to a
one-time program
[
193
]
. The resulting TO-LHS assemblage
violates GYNI, hence is not NS-LHS, but it is also supra-quantum,
since no quantum state can violate the GYNI inequality.

To find
a quantum-realizable assemblage that belongs to TO-LHS, but not to
NS-LHS, we take inspiration from Bancal
et al

[
136
]
,
who have found Bell behaviors obtainable from noisy
W
states with
the analogous DI-scenario property (TO-LHV, but not NS-LHV). A pure

W
state is given by
|W\rangle:=(|001\rangle+|010\rangle+|100\rangle)/\sqrt{3}
,
its noisy version with visibility
v
, by

\rho_{W}=v\ |W\rangle\langle W|+(1-v)\ \mathbb{1}^{(ABC)}/8\ .

(270)

Alice and Bob make von Neumann measurements on the bases
\eta X+\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}Z

(
x
or
y=0
) and
\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}X-\eta Z
(
x
or
y=1
),
with
\eta\approx 0.97177
, which yields the assemblage

\sigma_{a,b|x,y}^{\text{noisy }W}=v\ \sigma_{a,b|x,y}^{W}+(1-v)\ \mathbb{1}^{C}/8\ ,

(271)

where
\sigma_{a,b|x,y}^{W}
is given in Table
4
.
These measurements, together with an appropriate measurement by Charlie,
yield in
[
136
]
a DI-inequality violation requiring minimal
visibility.

We obtain the optimal NS-LHS witness
\bm{W}=\{W_{abxy}\}_{a,b,x,y}

for
\sigma_{a,b|x,y}^{\text{noisy }W}
for
v=0.58
, i.e.
\bm{W}

satisfies the property

-1\leq\sum_{a,b,x,y}\Tr[W_{abxy}\,\sigma_{a,b|x,y}^{\text{NS-LHS}}]\leq 0

(272)

for every NS-LHS assemblage
\bm{\sigma}^{\text{NS-LHS}}
.
Its components
W_{abxy}
are given in Table
5
.
This witness is violated by
\sigma_{a,b|x,y}^{\text{noisy }W}
from

v\approx 0.58
onwards; for
v=0.64
, it returns 0.0301.

Non-normalized states
\sigma_{\lambda}
needed in Eq. (

\displaystyle\sigma_{a,b|x,y}^{\text{noisy W}}

Chapter D
Time evolution and CPF correlation for the decay of a two level system
in a bosonic bath

In this Appendix, it is shown the detailed calculation to get to the
time evolved state (Eq. (
175
) in the main text) and to
the expression of the CPF correlation for the different schemes (Eq.
(
177
) and Eq. (
178
) in the main text). It is also shown
in more details the relation between the experimental HWP angles and
the theoretical time evolution.

4
Solution of Eq. (
174
) for initial separable states

The dynamics of the composite system (qubit + bosonic environment)
is given by the total Hamiltonian (
172
) that can be regarded
as the sum of a free evolution term
H_{0}=\frac{\omega_{0}}{2}\sigma_{z}+\sum_{k}\omega_{k}b_{k}^{{\dagger}}b_{k}

with an interaction term
H_{I}=\sum_{k}(g_{k}\sigma_{+}b_{k}+g_{k}^{\ast}\sigma_{-}b_{k}^{{\dagger}})
.
In the interaction picture the total state satisfies the Schrödinger
equation (
174
) with the time dependent interaction Hamiltonian

H_{I}(t)=e^{iH_{0}t}H_{I}e^{-iH_{0}t}=\sum_{k}(g_{k}e^{i\omega_{0}t}\sigma_{+}e^{i\omega_{k}b_{k}^{{\dagger}}b_{k}}b_{k}e^{-i\omega_{k}b_{k}^{{\dagger}}b_{k}}+h.c.)
.
We assume that the this composite system is closed and initially in
a pure separable state
12
12

12

This is the case for the very initial state because the environment
starts to evolve from vacuum and the system is measured before each
step of time evolution.
. The commutator of the total number of excitations
N=\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}+\sum_{k}b_{k}^{\dagger}b_{b}

with the total Hamiltonian vanishes (
[H_{tot},N]=0
), therefore
this quantity is conserved. As in the initial instant we consider
that the system has at most one excitation and the environment is
in its vacuum state, then the state at time
t>0
must be the general
state

|\Psi_{t}\rangle=\Big[a(t)|\uparrow\rangle+b(t)|\downarrow\rangle+|\downarrow\rangle\sum_{k}c_{k}(t)b_{k}^{{\dagger}}\Big]|0\rangle,

(274)

which is the superposition of all possible composite states with at
most one excitation.

From Schrödinger equation, the coefficients evolves as

\frac{d}{dt}b(t)=0.

(275)

Therefore,
b(t)=b(0)=b.
In addition, it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}a(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\sum_{k}g_{k}\exp(+i\phi_{k}t)c_{k}(t),

(276)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}c_{k}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-ig_{k}^{\ast}\exp(-i\phi_{k}t)a(t),

(277)

where
\phi_{k}\equiv\omega_{0}-\omega_{k}.
Integrating the last
equation as

c_{k}(t)=c_{k}(0)-ig_{k}^{\ast}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\exp(-i\phi_{k}t^{\prime})a(t^{\prime}),

(278)

the evolution for
a(t)
becomes

\frac{d}{dt}a(t)=-\int_{0}^{t}f(t-t^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}-ig(t).

(279)

Here,
f(t)
defines the bath correlation

f(t)\equiv\sum_{k}|g_{k}|^{2}\exp(+i\phi_{k}t),

(280)

while the inhomogeneous term is

g(t)\equiv\sum_{k}g_{k}\exp(+i\phi_{k}t)c_{k}(0).

(281)

Defining the Green function
G(t)
by the evolution

\frac{d}{dt}G(t)=-\int_{0}^{t}f(t-t^{\prime})G(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime},

(282)

with
G(0)=1,
the coefficient
a(t)
can be written as

a(t)=G(t)a(0)-i\int_{0}^{t}G(t-t^{\prime})g(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}.

(283)

Evolution in the time interval
(0,t)

The total system is prepared in the initial state
|\Psi_{0}\rangle=(a|\uparrow\rangle+b|\downarrow\rangle)\otimes|0\rangle

. Imediatelly after the preparation, the OQS is measured and the composite
system’s state becomes
|\Psi_{0}^{x}\rangle=(a_{x}|\uparrow\rangle+b_{x}|\downarrow\rangle)\otimes|0\rangle
,
with new coefficients dependent on the measurement outcome
x
.

Thus, the initial conditions are

a(0)=a_{x},\ \ \ \ ,b(0)=b_{x}\ \ \;\ c_{k}(0)=0,

(284)

implying
g(t)=0
. The coefficients can be expressed as

a(t)=G(t)a_{x},\ \ \ \ \ \ c_{k}(t)=-ia_{x}g_{k}^{\ast}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\exp(-i\phi_{k}t^{\prime})G(t^{\prime}).

(285)

Evolution in the time interval
(t,t+\tau)

The measurement module
Y
is applied, projecting the OQS into one
of its energy eigenstates
|\uparrow\rangle
or
|\downarrow\rangle
.
Different initial conditions must be used for this second step of
time evolution , depending on this measurement result. The initial
state for this step is the projection of Eq. (
274
)
on
|\uparrow\rangle
or
|\downarrow\rangle
for
y=+1
or
y=-1
,
respectively.

First Initial conditions
: when
y=+1

\tilde{a}(0)=1,\ \ \ \;\tilde{b}(0)=0,\;\ \ \ \tilde{c}_{k}(0)=0,

(286)

which implies
\tilde{g}(\tau)=0,
if follows the solution

\tilde{a}(\tau)=G(\tau),

(287)

while from Eq. (
278
) we get

\tilde{c}_{k}(\tau)=-ig_{k}^{\ast}\int_{0}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\exp[-i\phi_{k}(\tau^{\prime}+t)]G(\tau^{\prime}).

(288)

These solutions are equivalent to the previous ones [Eq. (
285
)]
under the replacement
t\rightarrow\tau.

Second initial conditions
: If
y=-1
, the set of initial
conditions for this second step is given by

\tilde{a}^{\prime}(0)=0,\ \ \ \tilde{b}^{\prime}(0)=b_{x}/\sqrt{1-|G(t)|^{2}}\ \ \ \tilde{c}_{k}^{\prime}(0)=c_{k}(t)/\sqrt{1-|G(t)|^{2}}.

(289)

From Eq. (
278
) we write
c_{k}(t)=-ig_{k}^{\ast}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\exp(-i\phi_{k}t^{\prime})G(t^{\prime}).

Thus, Eq. (
281
) becomes

\displaystyle\tilde{g}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\frac{\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\sum_{k}|g_{k}|^{2}\exp[+i\phi_{k}(\tau+t-t^{\prime})]G(t^{\prime})}{\sqrt{1-|G(t)|^{2}}},

(290)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-i}{\sqrt{1-|G(t)|^{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}f(\tau+t-t^{\prime})G(t^{\prime}).

From Eq. (
283
),
\tilde{a}^{\prime}(\tau)=-i\int_{0}^{\tau}G(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\tilde{g}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime},

delivering

\tilde{a}^{\prime}(\tau)=\frac{-\int_{0}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}f(\tau^{\prime}+t-t^{\prime})G(\tau-\tau^{\prime})G(t^{\prime})}{\sqrt{1-|G(t)|^{2}}},

(291)

which can be rewritten as

\tilde{a}^{\prime}(\tau)=\frac{-G(t,\tau)}{\sqrt{1-|G(t)|^{2}}}.

(292a)

This equation defines the function
G(t,\tau).
Moreover, from Eq.
(
278
), the other coefficients read

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{k}^{\prime}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-|G(t)|^{2}}}\Big\{c_{k}(t)+ig_{k}^{\ast}

(292b)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\exp[-i\phi_{k}(\tau^{\prime}+t)]G(\tau^{\prime},t)\Big\}.

The function
G(t,\tau),
after a change of integration variables
in Eq. (
291
), can be written as

G(t,\tau)=\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}f(\tau^{\prime}+t^{\prime})G(t-t^{\prime})G(\tau-\tau^{\prime}).

(293)

Eq. (
292
) shows that
G(t,\tau)
measures
the probability of finding the system in the upper state at time
\tau
given
that at the initial time
t
it was in the ground state

[Eq. (
289
)].

5
Calculation of the CPF correlation

Here we explicitly calculate the CPF correlation defined as:

C_{pf}(t,\tau)|_{y}=\langle O_{z}O_{x}\rangle_{y}-\langle O_{z}\rangle_{y}\langle O_{x}\rangle_{y}.

(294)

Equivalently,
C_{pf}(t,\tau)|_{y}=\sum_{zx}O_{z}O_{x}[P(z,x|y)-P(z|y)P(x|y)],

for different possible measurement schemes. The conditional values
explicitly read

\langle O_{x}\rangle_{y}=\sum_{x=\pm 1}xP(x|y),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \langle O_{z}\rangle_{y}=\sum_{z=\pm 1}zP(z|y),

(295)

and

\langle O_{z}O_{x}\rangle_{y}=\sum_{z,x=\pm 1}zxP(z,x|y).

(296)

Furthermore,
P(z|y)=\sum_{x=\pm 1}P(z,x|y),
and
P(x|y)=\sum_{z=\pm 1}P(z,x|y).

Measurement outcomes are indicated by
x,

y,
and
z,
while
directions in Bloch sphere are given by the eigenvectors of the Pauli
matrices,
\sigma_{x},

\sigma_{y},
and
\sigma_{z}.

5.1
First scheme, measurements
\sigma_{z}-\sigma_{z}-\sigma_{z}

The three measurements necessary to obtain the CPF correlations are
performed in in the same
\sigma_{z}-
direction, with corresponding
measurement projectors
\Pi_{+1}=|\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow|
and

\Pi_{-1}=|\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow|
. The initial condition
is taken as

|\Psi_{0}\rangle=(a|\uparrow\rangle+b|\downarrow\rangle)\otimes|0\rangle.

(297)

After the first
x
-measurement (measurement in the past), the total
state suffers the transformation
|\Psi_{0}\rangle\rightarrow|\Psi_{0}^{x}\rangle=\Pi_{\hat{z}=x}|\Psi_{0}\rangle/\sqrt{\langle\Psi_{0}|\Pi_{\hat{z}=x}|\Psi_{0}\rangle}

delivering
(x=\pm 1)

|\Psi_{0}^{x}\rangle=|x\rangle\otimes|0\rangle,

(298)

where we disregarded a global phase contribution. The probability
of each option
P(x)=\langle\Psi_{0}|\Pi_{\hat{z}=x}|\Psi_{0}\rangle,

reads

P(x=+1)=|a|^{2},\ \ \ \ \ P(x=-1)=|b|^{2}.

(299)

After the x-measurement, the system and environment evolve with the
Hamiltonian dynamics during a time interval
t,

|\Psi_{0}^{x}\rangle\rightarrow|\Psi_{t}^{x}\rangle.

We get,

\begin{tabular}[]{cccc}$x$&\vline&$|\Psi_{t}^{x}\rangle$&\vline\\
\hline\cr$+$&\vline&$[a(t)|\uparrow\rangle+|\downarrow\rangle\sum_{k}c_{k}(t)b_{k}^{{\dagger}}]|0\rangle$&\vline\\
$-$&\vline&$|\downarrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle$&\vline\end{tabular},

(300)

with
a(0)=1,

c_{k}(0)=0
and normalization
|a(t)|^{2}+\sum_{k}|c_{k}(t)|=1.

Thus, from Eq. (
284
), these coefficients are explicitly
given by Eq. (
285
).

Posteriorly, the second
y
-measurement, correspondent to the present,
is performed. The conditional probability of outcomes
y,
given
the previous outcomes
x,
is given by
P(y|x)=\langle\Psi_{t}^{x}|\Pi_{\hat{z}=y}|\Psi_{t}^{x}\rangle.

The joint probability of both outcomes is
P(y,x)=P(y|x)P(x).
The
retrodicted probability of past outcomes given the present ones is

P(x|y)=P(y,x)/P(y),
where
P(y)=\sum_{x}P(y,x).
We get

\begin{tabular}[]{ccccccccc}$y$&$x$&\vline&$P(y|x)$&\vline&$P(y,x)$&\vline&$P(x|y)$&\vline\\
\hline\cr$+$&$+$&\vline&$|a(t)|^{2}$&\vline&$|G(t)|^{2}|a|^{2}$&\vline&$1$&\vline\\
$+$&$-$&\vline&$0$&\vline&$0$&\vline&$0$&\vline\\
$-$&$+$&\vline&$1-|a(t)|^{2}$&\vline&$(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}$&\vline&$\frac{(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}}{(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}$&\vline\\
$-$&$-$&\vline&$1$&\vline&$|b|^{2}$&\vline&$\frac{|b|^{2}}{(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}$&\vline\end{tabular}.

(301)

After the second measurement, the total state suffer the transformation

|\Psi_{t}^{x}\rangle\rightarrow|\Psi_{t}^{yx}\rangle=\Pi_{\hat{z}=y}|\Psi_{t}^{x}\rangle/\sqrt{\langle\Psi_{t}^{x}|\Pi_{\hat{z}=y}|\Psi_{t}^{x}\rangle}.

Posteriorly, starting at time
t,

|\Psi_{t}^{yx}\rangle
evolves
with the total unitary dynamics during a time interval
\tau,
leading
to the transformation
|\Psi_{t}^{yx}\rangle\rightarrow|\Psi_{t+\tau}^{yx}\rangle.

From Eq. (
300
) the states conditioned to the output of
each measurement are

\begin{tabular}[]{ccccccc}$y$&$x$&\vline&$|\Psi_{t}^{yx}\rangle$&\vline&$|\Psi_{t+\tau}^{yx}\rangle$&\vline\\
\hline\cr$+$&$+$&\vline&$|\uparrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle$&\vline&$[\tilde{a}(\tau)|\uparrow\rangle+|\downarrow\rangle\sum_{k}\tilde{c}_{k}(\tau)b_{k}^{{\dagger}}]|0\rangle$&\vline\\
$+$&$-$&\vline&$\nexists$&\vline&$\nexists$&\vline\\
$-$&$+$&\vline&$|\downarrow\rangle\frac{\sum_{k}c_{k}(t)b_{k}^{{\dagger}}|0\rangle}{\sqrt{1-|a(t)|^{2}}}$&\vline&$[\tilde{a}^{\prime}(\tau)|\uparrow\rangle+|\downarrow\rangle\sum_{k}\tilde{c}_{k}^{\prime}(\tau)b_{k}^{{\dagger}}]|0\rangle$&\vline\\
$-$&$-$&\vline&$|\downarrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle$&\vline&$|\downarrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle$&\vline\end{tabular}.

(302)

The solution form
(y,x)=(+,+)
comes from Eq. (
286
)
[solutions (
)], while for
(y,x)=(-,+)
follows
from Eq. (
289
) [solutions (
292
)].

Finally, the third
z
-measurement is performed (measurement in the
future). The probability
P(z|yx)
of outcome
z
given the previous
outcomes
y
and
x,
is given by
P(z|yx)=\langle\Psi_{t+\tau}^{yx}|\Pi_{\hat{z}=z}|\Psi_{t+\tau}^{yx}\rangle.

The conditional probability of past and future event is
P(z,x|y)=P(z|y,x)P(x|y),

where
P(x|y)
follows from Eq. (
301
). We get

\begin{tabular}[]{cccccccc}$z$&$y$&$x$&\vline&$P(z|y,x)$&\vline&$P(z,x|y)$&\vline\\
\hline\cr$+$&$+$&$+$&\vline&$|\tilde{a}(\tau)|^{2}$&\vline&$|G(\tau)|^{2}$&\vline\\
$+$&$+$&$-$&\vline&$0$&\vline&$0$&\vline\\
$+$&$-$&$+$&\vline&$|\tilde{a}^{\prime}(\tau)|^{2}$&\vline&$\frac{|G(t,\tau)|^{2}|a|^{2}}{(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}$&\vline\\
$+$&$-$&$-$&\vline&$0$&\vline&$0$&\vline\\
$-$&$+$&$+$&\vline&$1-|\tilde{a}(\tau)|^{2}$&\vline&$1-|G(\tau)|^{2}$&\vline\\
$-$&$+$&$-$&\vline&$0$&\vline&$0$&\vline\\
$-$&$-$&$+$&\vline&$1-|\tilde{a}^{\prime}(\tau)|^{2}$&\vline&$\frac{(1-|G(t,\tau)|^{2}-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}}{(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}$&\vline\\
$-$&$-$&$-$&\vline&$1$&\vline&$\frac{|b|^{2}}{(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}$&\vline\end{tabular}.

(303)

The conditional probability of the last measurement follows from
P(z|y)=\sum_{x}P(z,x|y),

delivering

\begin{tabular}[]{ccccc}$z$&$y$&\vline&$P(z|y)$&\vline\\
\hline\cr$+$&$+$&\vline&$|G(\tau)|^{2}$&\vline\\
$+$&$-$&\vline&$\frac{|G(t,\tau)|^{2}|a|^{2}}{(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}$&\vline\\
$-$&$+$&\vline&$1-|G(\tau)|^{2}$&\vline\\
$-$&$-$&\vline&$\frac{(1-|G(t,\tau)|^{2}-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}{(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}$&\vline\end{tabular}.

(304)

From Eqs. (
301
) and (
304
), the expectation
values [Eqs. (
295
) and (
296
)] read

\langle O_{x}\rangle_{y=1}=1,\ \ \ \ \ \ \langle O_{z}\rangle_{y=1}=2|G(\tau)|^{2}-1,

(305)

while from Eq. (
303
) we get

\langle O_{z}O_{x}\rangle_{y=1}=2|G(\tau)|^{2}-1.

(306)

Thus, it follows

C_{pf}(t,\tau)|_{y=+1}=0.

(307)

On the other hand, for
y=-1,
the averages read

\langle O_{x}\rangle_{y=-1}=\frac{(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}-|b|^{2}}{(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}},

(308)

while

\langle O_{z}\rangle_{y=-1}=\frac{(2|G(t,\tau)|^{2}+|G(t)|^{2}-1)|a|^{2}-|b|^{2}}{(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}},

(309)

and

\langle O_{z}O_{x}\rangle_{y=-1}=\frac{(2|G(t,\tau)|^{2}+|G(t)|^{2}-1)|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}{(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}.

(310)

The CPF correlation then is

C_{pf}(t,\tau)|_{y=-1}\underset{\hat{z}\hat{z}\hat{z}}{=}\left\{\frac{4|a|^{2}|b|^{2}}{[(1-|G(t)|^{2})|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}]^{2}}\right\}|G(t,\tau)|^{2}.

(311)

5.2
Second scheme, x̂-ẑ-x̂

In this scheme, the first and last measurements are performed in
\hat{x}-
direction,
with measurement projector
\Pi_{\hat{x}+1}=|+\rangle\langle+|,

and
\Pi_{\hat{x}-1}=|-\rangle\langle-|,
where
|\pm\rangle=(1/\sqrt{2})(|\uparrow\rangle\pm|\downarrow\rangle).

The intermediate one is realized in
\hat{z}-
direction, with projector

\Pi_{\hat{z}=+1}
and
\Pi_{\hat{z}=-1}
defined above. The initial
system-environment state is

|\Psi_{0}\rangle=(a|\uparrow\rangle+b|\downarrow\rangle)\otimes|0\rangle.

(312)

After the first
x
-measurement
|\Psi_{0}\rangle\rightarrow|\Psi_{0}^{x}\rangle=\Pi_{\hat{x}=x}|\Psi_{0}\rangle/\sqrt{\langle\Psi_{0}|\Pi_{\hat{x}=x}|\Psi_{0}\rangle},

the bipartite state is

|\Psi_{0}^{x}\rangle=\frac{|\uparrow\rangle+x|\downarrow\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}\otimes|0\rangle,

(313)

where global phase contributions are disregarded. The probability
of each option
(x=\pm 1)

P(x)=\langle\Psi_{0}|\Pi_{\hat{x}=x}|\Psi_{0}\rangle,

reads

P(x)=\frac{1}{2}|a+xb|^{2}.

(314)

After the previous step,
|\Psi_{0}^{x}\rangle
evolves with the
unitary evolution during a time interval
t,

|\Psi_{0}^{x}\rangle\rightarrow|\Psi_{t}^{x}\rangle.

Using the initial conditions (
284
) and their associated
solution (
285
), we get

|\Psi_{t}^{x}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big[a(t)|\uparrow\rangle+x|\downarrow\rangle+|\downarrow\rangle\sum_{k}c_{k}(t)b_{k}^{{\dagger}}\Big]|0\rangle,

(315)

where
a(0)=1
and
c_{k}(0)=0.

Posteriorly, the second
y
-measurement is performed. The conditional
probability for the outcomes is
P(y|x)=\langle\Psi_{t}^{x}|\Pi_{\hat{z}=y}|\Psi_{t}^{x}\rangle,

which deliver

P(+|x)=\frac{|a(t)|^{2}}{2},\ \ \ \ \ \ P(-|x)=1-\frac{|a(t)|^{2}}{2},

(316)

where we used
|a(t)|^{2}+\sum_{k}|c_{k}(t)|^{2}=1.
This result
indicates that the random variable
y
is statistically independent
of
x,

P(y|x)=P(y).
Thus, the joint probability for the first
and second outcomes is
P(y,x)=P(y|x)P(x)=P(y)P(x).
The retrodicted
probability
P(x|y)=P(y,x)/P(y),
where
P(y)=\sum_{x}P(y,x),
becomes

P(x|y)=P(x).

(317)

After the second measurement, the state suffers the transformation

|\Psi_{t}^{x}\rangle\rightarrow|\Psi_{t}^{yx}\rangle=\Pi_{\hat{z}=y}|\Psi_{t}^{x}\rangle/\sqrt{\langle\Psi_{t}^{x}|\Pi_{\hat{z}=y}|\Psi_{t}^{x}\rangle}.

From Eq. (
315
), for
y=+1
we get

|\Psi_{t}^{+,x}\rangle=|\uparrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle,

(318)

while for
y=-1,

|\Psi_{t}^{-,x}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-|a(t)|^{2}}}|\downarrow\rangle\otimes\Big[x+\sum_{k}c_{k}(t)b_{k}^{{\dagger}}\Big]|0\rangle.

(319)

Starting at time
t,

|\Psi_{t}^{yx}\rangle
evolves with the total
unitary dynamics during a time interval
\tau,
leading to the transformation

|\Psi_{t}^{yx}\rangle\rightarrow|\Psi_{t+\tau}^{yx}\rangle.
From
Eq. (
318
) we get

|\Psi_{t+\tau}^{+,x}\rangle=\Big[\tilde{a}(\tau)|\uparrow\rangle+|\downarrow\rangle\sum_{k}\tilde{c}_{k}(\tau)b_{k}^{{\dagger}}\Big]|0\rangle,

(320)

with
\tilde{a}(0)=1,

\tilde{c}_{k}(0)=0
[Eq. (
286
)],
with
|\tilde{a}(\tau)|^{2}+\sum\nolimits_{k}|\tilde{c}_{k}(\tau)|^{2}=1.

Thus,
\tilde{a}(\tau)
and
\tilde{c}_{k}(\tau)
are given by Eq. (
).
On the other hand, from Eq. (
319
), it follows

\displaystyle|\Psi_{t+\tau}^{-,x}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{x|\downarrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle}{\sqrt{2-|a(t)|^{2}}}+\sqrt{\frac{1-|a(t)|^{2}}{2-|a(t)|^{2}}}

(321)

\displaystyle\times\Big[\tilde{a}^{\prime}(\tau)|\uparrow\rangle+|\downarrow\rangle\sum_{k}\tilde{c}_{k}^{\prime}(\tau)b_{k}^{{\dagger}}\Big]|0\rangle,

where
\tilde{a}^{\prime}(0)=0
and
\tilde{c}_{k}^{\prime}(0)=c_{k}(t)/\sqrt{1-|a(t)|^{2}}

[Eq. (
289
)] with
|\tilde{a}^{\prime}(\tau)|^{2}+\sum\nolimits_{k}|\tilde{c}_{k}^{\prime}(\tau)|^{2}=1.

In this case,
\tilde{a}^{\prime}(\tau)
and
\tilde{c}_{k}^{\prime}(\tau)

are then given by Eq. (
292
).

At the final stage, the third
z
-measurement is performed, where
the corresponding conditional probability reads
P(z|yx)=\langle\Psi_{t+\tau}^{yx}|\Pi_{\hat{x}=z}|\Psi_{t+\tau}^{yx}\rangle.

From the previous expressions, we get

P(z|+,x)=\frac{1}{2},

(322)

while

P(z|-,x)=\frac{1}{2}\Big[1-zx\frac{G(t,\tau)+G^{\ast}(t,\tau)}{2-|G(t)|^{2}}\Big].

(323)

The CPF probability
P(z,x|y)=P(z|y,x)P(x|y),
from the previous
two expressions and Eq. (
317
), reads
(y=+1)

P(z,x|+)=\frac{1}{2}P(x)=\frac{1}{4}|a+xb|^{2},

(324)

while
(y=-1)

P(z,x|-)=\frac{|a+xb|^{2}}{4}\Big[1-zx\frac{G(t,\tau)+G^{\ast}(t,\tau)}{2-|G(t)|^{2}}\Big].

(325)

From Eqs. (
324
) and (
325
), the conditional
expectation values [Eqs. (
295
) and (
296
)]
for
y=+1
read

\langle O_{x}\rangle_{y=+1}=2\mathrm{Re}(ab^{\ast}),\ \ \ \ \ \ \langle O_{z}\rangle_{y=+1}=0,

(326)

and

\langle O_{z}O_{x}\rangle_{y=+1}=0,

(327)

which implies

C_{pf}(t,\tau)|_{y=+1}=0.

(328)

On the other hand, for
y=-1,
the averages read

\langle O_{x}\rangle_{y=-1}=2\mathrm{Re}(ab^{\ast}),

(329)

while

\langle O_{z}\rangle_{y=-1}=-2\mathrm{Re}(ab^{\ast})\frac{G(t,\tau)+G^{\ast}(t,\tau)}{2-|G(t)|^{2}}.

(330)

Furthermore,

\langle O_{z}O_{x}\rangle_{y=-1}=-\frac{G(t,\tau)+G^{\ast}(t,\tau)}{2-|G(t)|^{2}}.

(331)

The CPF correlation then is

C_{pf}(t,\tau)|_{y=-1}\underset{\hat{x}\hat{z}\hat{x}}{=}-\left\{\frac{1-[2\mathrm{Re}(ab^{\ast})]^{2}}{1-|G(t)|^{2}/2}\right\}\mathrm{Re}[G(t,\tau)].

(332)

For a
\ \hat{y}-\hat{z}-\hat{y}
measurements scheme, by performing
a similar calculation, the CPF correlation reads

C_{pf}(t,\tau)|_{y=-1}\underset{\hat{y}\hat{z}\hat{y}}{=}-\left\{\frac{1-[2\mathrm{Im}(ab^{\ast})]^{2}}{1-|G(t)|^{2}/2}\right\}\mathrm{Re}[G(t,\tau)].

(333)

6
Map representation of the total unitary dynamics

For experimental implementation, the system is encoded in the light
polarization states, while the bath is effectively implemented through
different spatial light modes
[
168
,
169
]
.

The total unitary evolution in first interval
(0,t)
can be written
as the map

\displaystyle|\downarrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle|\downarrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle,

(334a)

\displaystyle|\uparrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\cos(\theta)|\uparrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle+\sin(\theta)|\downarrow\rangle\otimes|1\rangle,\ \ \ \

(334b)

where here
|0\rangle
and
|1\rangle
represent spatial modes that
respectively take into account the absence or presence of one excitation
in the environment Bosonic modes. Thus, the angle
\theta
is given
by the relation

\cos(\theta)=a(t)=G(t),

(335)

where
a(t)
follows from Eq. (
285
).

In the interval
(t,t+\tau)
the total unitary dynamics realize the
following mapping

\displaystyle|\downarrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle|\downarrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle,

(336a)

\displaystyle|\uparrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\cos(\tilde{\theta})|\uparrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle+\sin(\tilde{\theta})|\downarrow\rangle\otimes|1\rangle,

(336b)

\displaystyle|\downarrow\rangle\otimes|1\rangle

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\sin(\tilde{\theta}^{\prime})|\uparrow\rangle\otimes|0\rangle+\cos(\tilde{\theta}^{\prime})|\downarrow\rangle\otimes|1\rangle.\ \ \ \

(336c)

The angles are given by the relations

\cos(\tilde{\theta})=\tilde{a}(\tau)=G(\tau),

(337)

and

\sin(\tilde{\theta}^{\prime})=\tilde{a}^{\prime}(\tau)=-\frac{G(t,\tau)}{\sqrt{1-|G(t)|^{2}}},

(338)

where
\tilde{a}(\tau)
and
\tilde{a}^{\prime}(\tau)
follows
from Eqs. (
285
) and (
292
) respectively.

From the previous mapping, it is possible to rewrite the CPF correlation
in terms of angle variables. From Eq. (
311
) we get

C_{pf}|_{y=-1}\underset{\hat{z}\hat{z}\hat{z}}{=}\left\{\frac{4|a|^{2}|b|^{2}}{[\sin^{2}(\theta)|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}]^{2}}\right\}\sin^{2}(\theta)\sin^{2}(\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}),

(339)

while from Eq. (
333
) it follows

C_{pf}|_{y=-1}\underset{\hat{x}\hat{z}\hat{x}}{=}\left\{\frac{1-[2\mathrm{Re}(ab^{\ast})]^{2}}{1-\cos^{2}(\theta)/2}\right\}\sin(\theta)\sin(\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}).

(340)

These two expressions do not depend on angle
\tilde{\theta}.
In
fact, this angle is relevant when
y=+1,
where
C_{pf}|_{y=+1}\underset{\hat{z}\hat{z}\hat{z}}{=}0

and
C_{pf}|_{y=+1}\underset{\hat{x}\hat{z}\hat{x}}{=}0.

The previous expressions for the CPF correlation in terms of angle
variables can also be derived from the measurement schemes and by
using the dynamical maps Eqs. (
334
) and (
336
).
For example, the CPF probability
P(z,x|y)
for the
\hat{z}-\hat{z}-\hat{z}

scheme [compare with Eq. (
303
)] reads

\begin{tabular}[]{cccccc}$z$&$y$&$x$&\vline&$P(z,x|y)$&\vline\\
\hline\cr$+$&$+$&$+$&\vline&$\cos^{2}(\tilde{\theta})$&\vline\\
$+$&$+$&$-$&\vline&$0$&\vline\\
$+$&$-$&$+$&\vline&$\frac{\sin^{2}(\theta)\sin^{2}(\tilde{\theta}^{\prime})|a|^{2}}{\sin^{2}(\theta)|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}$&\vline\\
$+$&$-$&$-$&\vline&$0$&\vline\\
$-$&$+$&$+$&\vline&$\sin^{2}(\tilde{\theta})$&\vline\\
$-$&$+$&$-$&\vline&$0$&\vline\\
$-$&$-$&$+$&\vline&$\frac{\sin^{2}(\theta)\cos^{2}(\tilde{\theta}^{\prime})|a|^{2}}{\sin^{2}(\theta)|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}$&\vline\\
$-$&$-$&$-$&\vline&$\frac{|b|^{2}}{\sin^{2}(\theta)|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}}$&\vline\end{tabular}.

(341)

For the
\hat{x}-\hat{z}-\hat{x}
scheme [compare with Eqs. (
324
)
and (
325
)] it can be written as
(y=+1)

P(z,x|+)=\frac{1}{4}|a+xb|^{2},

(342)

while
(y=-1)

P(z,x|-)=\frac{1}{4}|a+xb|^{2}\Big[1+2zx\frac{\sin(\theta)\sin(\tilde{\theta}^{\prime})}{2-\cos(\theta)}\Big].

(343)
